ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u132	Dear President, kind senators and kind senators, the European Council that will take place on Thursday and Friday is the first ordinary Council in the presence, after two extraordinary meetings, also held in presence: that of 17-21 July, the famous negotiation, when the historic agreement on Next generation EU and the new multiannual financial framework was reached; that of 1 and 2 October, which recovered the European Council of mid-March last, which had been postponed because of the Covid-19. The progress of the current European Council in mid-October will have to strengthen the awareness that the full overcoming of the Covid-19 at the health and economic level remains, more than ever before, the priority objective for the European Union and its governments. It is in this spirit, in the spirit of genuine intra-European solidarity, that the meeting at which the European Council will be called upon to examine the epidemiological situation, to address the overall coordination of interventions and to discuss, at this stage, the development and distribution of a vaccine at EU level. In line with what I myself and the members of the government have always supported on the European tables since the beginning of the emergency from Covid-19, I will confirm the belief that national measures aimed at the containment of contagion must rely on a European framework of effective health and economic support and in the field of health protection and border management respectful of national competences. The Italian commitment to the fight against the pandemic is authoritative and concrete confirmation in the decision taken together with the President of the European Commission Ursula von der Leyen to organize here in Italy a Global Health Summit during our Presidency of the G20 next year, 2021. This is a crucial opportunity to enhance our leadership on global health issues and the fight against the Covid-19, which is also explicitly recognised by the World Health Organization. The Government's efforts in the various European and international fora since the beginning of this pandemic have also focused on the search for an effective vaccine, with the aim of its fair distribution within and outside the European Union, in particular with regard to the external dimension for the benefit of the most vulnerable countries. Europe is called to remain united. Together we must be as determined as possible in this phase of the continental resurgence of the Covid-19 and the hoped-for revival of our economy. After giving an ambitious and timely response to the pandemic crisis, we on the European side must continue to work swiftly on the regulatory implementation of the Next Generation EU programme. All Member States must act consistently and loyally in accordance with the political commitment made by the European Council decision of 21 July. It is a moral obligation first of all not to forget the thousands of victims of the pandemic and to offer a better perspective to the new generations of Europeans. We therefore continue to support the efforts made by the summits of the Community institutions and the German Presidency of the Council of the European Union to avoid delays in the operation of Next generation EU and the new multiannual financial framework. In particular, we must not allow delays to occur due to the divisive use of principles and rules, such as those relating to the rule of law on which the European Council adopted its decisions on 21 July. This project to relaunch the European economy is an unprecedented opportunity to bring Italy back on a path of growth and sustainable, fair and inclusive development. In this regard, I would like to express my thanks to the Senators; sincere and heartfelt thanks for the report prepared by the European Union's budgetary and policy committees on the National Recovery and Resilience Plan, which contains valuable indications for the drafting, implementation and monitoring of the Plan. The dual challenge posed by the digital ecological transition is inevitably a central priority for the development of the Plan. In particular, 40% of the resources will be devoted to investments in environmental sustainability, which help the country to achieve the emission reduction targets identified by the Integrated National Energy and Climate Plan and at the same time promote new employment, innovation and development opportunities. A crucial attention will be devoted to the valorization of Italian primacy in the field of circular economy (a primacy of which we must all be proud), to the energy efficiency of public and private buildings, with the extension of the measures already in force, such as the building superbonus, and to the relaunch of crucial investments in the protection of the territory and the water heritage. In addition, at least 20% of the Plan's resources will be devoted to the objective of digitalising innovation and the competitiveness of the production system. In addition to relaunching and refining the existing measures - I am thinking of the 4.0 transition package - a particular attention will be paid to the fundamental increase in digital skills of both public administration and the productive world, and to investments aimed at filling digital gaps in fixed, but also mobile connectivity, which still characterize many areas of the country. We can no longer tolerate that our country is at the last places in the European rankings related to the digitisation of the economy and society itself. The objective of the Government in this area is to build a more innovative Italy, simpler and more and more prepared to seize the opportunities offered by digital technologies, crucial for the full social and economic development of future generations. In addition, the Government shares the call to focus available resources on a limited number of qualifying projects that meet the strategic objectives of the Next Generation EU package. In this regard, the Government is developing the six missions - in which, as you have seen, the Plan is organized and which are indicated in the guidelines that we have presented to you - in a limited number of actions, each time closing a specific gap in the country. These actions contain the qualifying projects that will be accompanied by the necessary indicators of progress and of the performance of the works: the baselines or the trend scenario; the targets that we intend to reach; the milestones, whose completion is necessary to obtain the successive tranches of financing. Each project contained in the actions is referred to an institutional entity identified as responsible for its implementation. And the Government - I have already mentioned this - is determined to provide for an ad hoc regulatory framework for governance, implementation and monitoring of the Plan, which will be properly submitted to Parliament. I will give the utmost attention to the proposal contained in the report - which we find an interesting idea - which aims to ensure appropriate incentives for the implementation of projects, as well as penalties for the inactivity of those who do not comply with the timetable set. [[Applause]] We grasp the spirit, the objective and the ratio of this proposal, which are absolutely shared by the Government in order to ensure fast and reliable times for the implementation of the Plan, which is essential to ensure a rapid absorption of European resources and to prevent that once again, as has happened in the past, they may be wasted. I hope that the climate of loyal cooperation between all the parliamentary groups, which has already existed in recent months during the toughest phase of the pandemic, can also be preserved in view of the vote on the next budgetary deviation, which is a fundamental step to ensure the resources necessary to face the challenges that unfortunately the pandemic still anticipates us. Maximum will also be at all stages, as is also hoped in the same report presented by the Commissions, the loyal collaboration with the Regions and with the local authorities. The objective of the Government is that all institutional actors involved in the implementation of the Plan, but also political forces, trade unions, businesses and civil society as a whole, can share the economic policy strategy that is prefigured in the Plan itself. We must all move in this same direction of relaunching the country. The other main points on the agenda of this European Council also concern issues that will have an impact on Europe in the coming decades. I am talking about the negotiations on the future relationship between the European Union and the United Kingdom; I am talking about climate change and relations between the European Union and Africa. As regards the negotiations with London, the European Council is called upon to examine the implementation of the withdrawal agreement and the state of negotiations on the future relationship with the United Kingdom, as well as to discuss preparations for all possible scenarios after 1 January 2021. As you know, we are at a crucial stage in this negotiation, both politically and technically. The President of the European Commission Ursula von der Leyen and British Prime Minister Boris Johnson, on Saturday 3 October, invited their chief negotiators to make an effort to bridge the continuing gap between the parties. This distance concerns above all, but not only (I would like to stress this), the central themes of fisheries, equal conditions (the famous level playing field) and the governance of the future report. Now more than ever it is essential, as Italy has always consistently supported, to maintain the unity of the 27 Member States in support of the European Commission and its chief negotiator, Michel Barnier. Time is running, negotiating session after negotiating session, towards that deadline, which, as mentioned, is set at 31 December this year, for an agreement capable of regulating the relationship between the European Union and the United Kingdom. The time that remains is not so much, indeed very little, and must be used by both sides wisely and also with political foresight. To a scenario without agreement, the so-called no deal, we want and must continue to prefer - we will do so until the last useful moment - an agreement - but not at all cost - balanced and fair. We want an ambitious partnership with the United Kingdom, a future relationship that reflects both the depth of relations and the current economic, political and social balance between the United Kingdom and one of the major global markets, such as the European Union. It is equally clear that the European Union should continue to support the restoration by London of the full implementation of the withdrawal agreement which we consider to be violated by the British internal market bill, which, as you know, is the subject of an infringement procedure by the European Commission. Respect for the Protocol on Ireland is, in fact, a fundamental international legal obligation to protect peace and stability on the island of Ireland, as well as a prerequisite for the successful outcome of negotiations on the future partnership. Moving on to climate change, a debate will take place at the European Council in preparation for the political decisions that will hopefully be taken at the next European Council in December. We are confident that this sequence will start, from the European Council on Thursday and Friday, from an ambitious debate. The pandemic has opened up new scenarios, also for the strategy of combating climate change and environmental protection, essential for a reconstruction that truly rests on new foundations. It will therefore be necessary to work together to ensure that the re-launching of ambitions in climate matters increasingly meets the demands of our citizens (I am thinking above all of the younger generations) for a resilient future to climate change and efficient use of our resources, which are limited. In this regard, I would like to reiterate that a timely launch of Next Generation EU and the new Multiannual Financial Framework is also essential to honour climate change commitments. These are commitments to be included in a legal and financial framework that avoids a zero-sum game between the Member States closest to achieving the climate objectives and those that remain behind. Italy has the ambition to be at the forefront of Europe, but also in a broader perspective, in promoting a high level of ambition on a global challenge such as climate change. This, moreover, is what characterizes our partnership with the United Kingdom for COP26. You know that this event has been postponed from the current year to the next year and we are co-chairs with the United Kingdom. This is also the line that will characterize the programme of the Italian Presidency of the G20, which will be articulated around the trinomial: people, planet, prosperity. The message that we want to convey and on which we want to invite all world leaders to reflect is simple: we need to take care of the planet and people with a holistic approach, aimed at the objective of a substantial economic and social recovery, but that at the same time is inclusive and truly sustainable. [[Applause]] Finally, at its meeting on external relations, the European Council will adopt conclusions on relations between the European Union and Africa. This is a further political signal to confirm the European commitment to a partnership with Africa that is broad, multi-dimensional and multi-level. The basic objective must remain that of partnership between equals, modifying the paradigm of substantial asymmetry on which our relations with the African continent have historically been founded. It is also appropriate - and we are working in this respect - that the partnership between the European Union and the African Union should also be reflected in better cooperation on migration matters and in consultations that can promote solutions to regional crises on the African continent. Thank you for your attention. [[Applause.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u136	Mr President, Conte, ladies and gentlemen, climate change has taken place over the last decade to the attention of the agenda of the governments of the most industrialized countries, becoming one of the most important topics on the political agenda. In fact, climate change appears to be a real, significant, global phenomenon that directly invests many fundamental fields of existence, from the environment to health, from the economy to the social aspects. Addressing the phenomenon from an active perspective, assessing the impact of human activity on the environment, appears to be the only way forward to try to contain the devastating effects of this phenomenon on human life and activities. That climate change is of human origin has also demonstrated this serious period that unfortunately we are living because of the coronavirus. In fact, the result of global pollution reduction that we have experienced in lockdown, having been a forced stop to the industry, the circulation of cars and other pollutants, is proof of how this climate change is directly influenced by human activities. The numerous scientific studies, starting with those prepared by the UN Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), also tell us. Ban Ki-moon himself emphasized this in his December 2007 speech when he became Secretary General of the United Nations, raising the problem worldwide for the first time. These climatic variations, I would stress, have a serious and in many ways dangerous impact on a strategic sector for human life and for the existence of what is agriculture. The effects of climate change on this sector are due to three macro-areas: agricultural production, food chain security and social security. Some research models show that agricultural production, measured in monetary terms, is a function of some climatic variables: the change in climate order, in fact, will change - and is already changing - the behaviour of the main variables affecting production, such as temperatures, which have already increased and will increase by 1,5-2 degrees Celsius until 2050, and precipitation, which are already in sharp decline. As regards the second macro-area, that of food chain security, the expected impacts of climate change will mainly concern the increased criticality of water management and the acceleration of the spread of diseases and contamination in agricultural products and food. The lack of water will have a serious impact on agricultural production and the European landscape. Finally, with regard to the third macro-area, which concerns social security, studies tell us that the effects of climate change on the availability and distribution of natural resources will most likely lead to an increase in turbulence and migration. The competition for natural resources, which in the coming decades will be aggravated by global warming, will most likely produce turbulence and migratory phenomena in various regions of the country. Numerous effects of these changes have already been observed in nature: the advance of the flowering period of the plants, the lengthening of the growing season of the vineyards and the changes in other natural cycles of the plants. Changes in the timetable of cultivation activities, such as sowing and harvesting, indicate that farmers are unfortunately already adapting to new climatic conditions. The mitigation actions on which we must aim are potentially many, but the main ones, which must be brought out loud in Europe can be summarized in these areas: reduction of direct and indirect energy consumption; integration and diffusion of renewable energy sources; reduction of methane and nitrous oxide emissions produced by farms and fertilization practices; optimization of water use. Water is strategic for the future of humanity and, above all, for the change in cultivation practices and crop varieties that farmers are calling on us loudly. These varieties must necessarily adapt to climatic variations. Soil consumption must then be kept to a minimum, because all available soil must be used for food production. The global trend leads us to move towards organic farming and to find methods. This is one of the demands that comes, for example, from the agricultural world to protect niche productions, those excellences that are already positioned in the market. Mr President, the agricultural sector is called upon to provide solutions that are capable of increasing the adaptation of crops to the direct effects of climate change, as well as finding solutions that contribute to the reduction of the gas emissions that are characteristic of agriculture. We know that agriculture and, above all, intensive farms contribute 30% to the pollution of the entire planet. The urgency of decisive and immediate action to reverse this process is therefore in no way postponed and in this emerging context Italy has and must take a leading role in the issues of climate change, landscape protection and soil protection. The green new deal included in the government program - because we have it as a fundamental point of our programme - goes in this direction, trying to arrive at a radical change of cultural paradigm that leads to the inclusion of the protection of the environment and biodiversity as the fundamental principles of our constitutional system. In the right direction are also the recent measures, which have always been put in place by the Government, on reforestation, including the provisions of the budget law, which provided for measures which are equally important for the environmental transition. The Common Agricultural Policy (CAP), to which no funds must be taken (Mr President, there must be no cut in the CAP funds and we must defend the money that has been allocated to it for the other years), has put in place over time some operational instruments that can potentially help reduce the exposure and vulnerability of the agricultural production system to the effects of climate change and increase resilience. Same thing you are trying to achieve through the Rural Development Plans and measures in favour of forestation. Other new tools are needed, also because the coronavirus pandemic has created other inconveniences in such a strategic sector for our nation. On the occasion of the coronavirus we have touched with hand the strategicity of this field and of all the operators of the field, that they have been able to guarantee the necessary supplyings in such a difficult period. That is why, Mr President, I call on you, for agriculture, to pay great attention to the distribution of the money from the recovery fund. Only in this way can this dangerous tendency to leave a planet devastated and without food be reversed. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u142	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, Mr President-in-Office of the Council, at the European Council on 15 and 16 October, one of the items on the agenda will be the issue of climate change and its consequences on the planet. Above all, we will talk about the progress being made towards the European Union's objective of achieving climate neutrality by 2050, including through the adoption of the 2030 climate targets plan. For too long we have lived, made economy, produced, used fossil sources and exploited the resources of the planet as if this were inexhaustible and capable of absorbing any variation induced by human activities. At last, after decades of alarm launched from several parts - and from the Movimento 5 Stars, among others - it has been noted that these modalities of development are unsustainable and incompatible with the environment and that, if we continue on this road, we would end up inexorably in the abyss, long before the worst forecasts that only ten or twenty years ago did some climatologists and some exponents of both civil society and politics. This new awareness of the extreme gravity of the moment and of the need to intervene drastically is found in the list of the six missions that have been contemplated in the recovery plan and that - I remember - are digitalization and innovation, infrastructures for mobility, education and training, social equity of gender and territorial, health, green revolution and ecological transition. Just recently, the Senate voted on the report on guidelines for the definition of the National Recovery and Resilience Plan, which will use the recovery fund. Thanks to these huge funds, which amount to 750 billion, of which 209 for Italy, and to the philosophy that inspires its operation, we are able, perhaps for the first time, to plan the future of the environment, infrastructure and sustainable development of Italy and Europe for the next fifty years. Without this instrument, we would never have had such a perspective and I believe it is right for everyone and intellectually honest to admit that having obtained it - and this is the first time it has happened in the European Union - is a huge achievement, thanks to this Government and the constant stimulus exerted within it by the 5 Star Movement. A few minutes ago, an opposition senator said he wanted to make a truth operation: according to him, the 209 billion of the recovery would have arrived thanks to a mere arithmetic calculation; above all, they would have arrived with any Italian government in office and therefore the action of the President of the Count Council, its Executive and its Ministers would not have affected in any way. If that is true, I wonder why this simple arithmetic calculation was not done in 2008 and the following years. How come, during that frightening crisis, which is also exogenous, like this one that we are unfortunately living, the then centre-right government, instead of doing the simple arithmetic calculation and returning from Europe with the funds, returned with the austerity and the letter of the European Central Bank that asked us to put the accounts in order, since the spread had splashed at 575 points? I ask myself and the House. I would like to remind everyone that, until the end of March 2020, so not a century ago, but only six months ago, the European response to the pandemic, now definitively exploded, consisted only of the famous - or infamous - package in which the principals of the European Investment Bank (EIB), the European Stability Mechanism (ESM) and the Support to mitigate unemployment risks in an emergency (Sure); packages that Government and Parliament, this time almost unanimously, had found insufficient and not up to the challenge that was emerging before the whole of the European Union. At the end of that month, in particular on the eve of the European Council on 26 March, the Italian Government took the lead in an initiative, condensed in a letter signed by eight other European countries, to ask the European Union for a totally new economic intervention, much more massive and finally supported by the issuance of a common debt. The initiative of our Government, as you know, has been able to speed up a debate that has led in a short time the European Commission to propose the recovery fund with the famous division into subsidies, that is, the funds lost (to Italy will touch about 80 billion, a colossal figure) and the part in loans, for about 127 billion, on facilitated conditions and with particularly long repayment times. Above all, the initiative of the Italian Government, continually pricked by the 5 Star Movement, has led the Community institutions to tie the recovery fund to crucial investment sectors for the future of Italy, such as digital, green, energy transition and intangible and material infrastructure. All the controversy, more or less instrumental and propaganda, and the true lies that have been spread over the last few months will not be able to obscure a fundamental point of the whole issue, namely that the recovery fund is a great result obtained by the Government, the country and the Parliament. As far as the time of arrival of the money from the recovery fund is concerned, which has also been mentioned in this House today - in my view - in terms of Italy's delays, the timing largely escapes the possibility of intervention by national governments for the simple, but very good reason that there is a well-established and well-defined European procedure, that dictates the path and that the National Plans for Recovery and Resilience can be presented for a first assessment by the Commission when the European Regulation on Recovery and Resilience Mechanism comes into force. This, presumably, will not happen before 1 January 2021; other than Italy's delays. Then the European Commission will have two months for its assessments and to propose to the Ecofin Council the approval of the various national plans. In turn, the Ecofin Council will have to approve the National Plans by means of an act to be adopted by qualified majority within four weeks. In any case, all other regulatory acts that will have to regulate the Next Generation EU are currently under negotiation. So there is no delay and those who are making a vicious propaganda, suggesting that Italy is late on the road map, falsely says, because the presentation of the National Plans, as I have just said, can only take place after the European adoption of the regulation to which I referred earlier. Another fandonia, which has been propalated in recent times, concerns the legend that the money of the recovery fund would arrive late and that this government would not know how to use them and brancoli in the dark and in the approximation. It is false, of course, because the recovery fund funds will arrive in 2021, and in fact the budget law, which will be presented to the Chambers in a few weeks, will use 25 billion of these resources, of which 14 of subsidies and 11 of loans. I do not say that, but it is written in NADEF that we are currently examining the budget in the Committee on Budgets. There's more. Today Italy is able to find resources on the markets at very advantageous rates. Five-year government bonds have virtually zero interest rates and shorter bonds even have negative interest rates. Our ten-year period in relation to the German one is 120 basis points spread and interest is 0.7%. There are levels that haven't really been reached in decades. In conclusion, the country is in good hands and we of Movimento 5 Stars will continue to operate in the exclusive interest of citizens, businesses and especially of those who have suffered and had little or nothing in recent years. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u146	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, Mr President-in-Office of the Council, tomorrow's European Council, among the various items on the agenda, also presents a number of very problematic international issues, which concern us and are of particular concern to us. I am thinking first of all of the issue of Brexit, then of the resumption of the agreements with Britain, aimed at averting a no deal, a hard Brexit. Let us look with concern at the development of the situation in the United Kingdom, which seems to be a blurry scenario, which we hoped would not have materialized. This is an urgent issue, which concerns us closely, also as a result of the presence of over 700,000 of our fellow countrymen living there and who are likely to see compromises on the rights acquired to date, both in terms of health care and rights in the world of work and study, and in everyday practical life. That is why we need the European Union to provide proper protection and to sit at the different consultation tables, making its voice heard even louder today. It is not an exaggeration to fear for our fellow countrymen, because the United Kingdom, with the current Prime Minister Johnson, has already repeatedly shown a selective and hostile attitude, set to advance the negotiations only in the steps of direct interest of Britain, without considering and denouncing the agreements already entered into with the European Union. Here, in addition to our fellow citizens, we must also protect our companies: there are about 30,000 Italian companies that live on export, protagonists of that trade surplus active between Italy and the United Kingdom of almost 22 billion euros per year. I am thinking, for example, of the producers and exporters of prosecco or agro-food, but also of many of our companies that, instead, turn to the field of defence or, in general, to the companies of Made in Italy that, without the adequate protection in the bilateral organization of export relations, risk being severely penalized by a hard Brexit. That is why - and it is important to reiterate this right now - it is essential that at national level we also think of extraordinary measures to be taken where a possible no deal should occur, even today in advance. Brexit was a choice of British citizens, legitimate and certainly sad, but it is important that we now do everything possible to ensure that its consequences do not fall on our European citizens and, even less, on our companies. Alongside the issue of Brexit, there are other aspects on the agenda of tomorrow's European Council which we consider to be particularly important. In fact, talking about Turkey, Belarus or Nagorno-Karabakh, as set out in the two-day agenda in Brussels, means discussing how to advance our deeply democratic European culture, whether it be human, social or civil rights; all rights which, at present, are strongly compromised, both in Turkey and in Belarus. When we read, for example, of the young Turkish lawyer Ebru Timtik, who protested against his arrest and demanded a fair trial, who died after 238 days of hunger strike; or when we see women in Belarus, who continue to be repressed while peacefully demonstrating against the recent blatant electoral fraud; when we learn of such news, we cannot simply express concern at the growing tensions in the eastern Mediterranean or Minsk or the resumption of the conflicts in Nagorno-Karabakh. Similarly, we cannot simply show a duty of solidarity with Greece or Cyprus for the provocative activities of drilling and attacking Greek ships by Turkey, as happened just a few hours ago, again. In view of the quality of these abuses, we as the European Union cannot turn to the other side. Europe is the largest area of protection of rights and commitment to peace and the stabilisation of peoples. On the one hand, if we are very pleased that the Council of Foreign Ministers decided yesterday to introduce sanctions against both Belarus and Russia, thanks to the agreements that have been reached, which will be ratified tomorrow by the European Council, at the same time, we believe that it is time to begin a thorough reflection on the position that the European Union should take in relation to countries which are stagnating in anti-democratic policies, in violation of the rules of international law. This, regardless of whether all Member States are in favour of it. We believe that it is positive and that President von der Leyen's proposal for a Magnitsky Act should be supported, that is to say a mechanism that removes the European capitals from the right to put vetoes to the European Union on foreign policy issues, introducing the possibility of a qualified majority decision, so as to offer greater flexibility to the Commission in imposing sanctions on certain countries in the event of human rights violations. Like Italy Viva, Mr President, we are convinced that the future of Italy is strongly anchored in Europe; a Europe that we want even more ambitious, more courageous; a Europe that can also say that rights come first and not after economic conveniences. ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u152	Mr President, it may surprise you that tomorrow the European Council, in addition to examining the epidemiological situation in the countries of the European Union, on which the President-in-Office of the Council spoke earlier, will also discuss the climate, Brexit and Africa. It is singular, because Africa, Brexit and climate seem too distant to be discussed at the same time; but it is also unique because, in the midst of the Covid-19 emergency and its health, economic and social consequences, one can well think that the priorities are others. On the other hand, the European Council's agenda is dramatically topical and puts us in front of major universal issues, which are very hot for Europe and Italy. And that is where I shall now say a few words. There is a political thread, strong and clearly visible, linking the climate, Africa and Brexit. Three factors that, to varying degrees, undermine the stability of the Planet, which is our most precious asset and our greatest guarantee of economic development, civil progress and even peace in the world. The little time we have left for this debate today, I say to the President of the Senate, is a clear political signal. It points to the modest position in our parliamentary hierarchy of issues such as climate change, Africa and Brexit. The Senate is no longer accustomed to major debates on issues that shake the world. Trump's policies, the role of Merkel in Europe, the climate, China's advance, the axis of the world moving from the Atlantic to the Pacific, the crisis of democracy and the expansion of authoritarian regimes. These are themes that will affect the next few decades and that are discussed elsewhere, never here in the Senate. In the minutes I've been allowed, I'm just gonna make some political considerations. In particular, I want to highlight the relationship that connects the showy backwardness of the planet's environmental balances with Africa and Brexit and that can be summarized as follows: their contribution to the destabilisation of the world is very important, so much so as to put at risk even the most precious asset we have, peace. Political, economic and social stability is the best guarantee of peace, while instability increases international tensions and eventually can lead to war, as is happening between Azerbaijani and Armenians for Nagorno-Karabakh, with behind the shadow of Russia and Turkey. Or as could happen in the Aegean Sea, a stone's throw from Italy, following tensions between Turkey (always Turkey) and Greece. It is enough to increase the earth's temperature by a few degrees to make life impossible in immense parts of sub-Saharan Africa, to cause violent breaks between the nations of those regions and to push entire populations to the North of the world and to Europe, without any force and any of our laws being able to stop them. They are women and men fleeing from certain death. What this means in terms of the instability of the planet, Europe is learning it day after day. It was the political changes of North Africa that brought the Turkish and Russian military fleets first to Syria and then to the Central Mediterranean, radically changing the political and military balances in a sea on which much of Europe's peace depends. It is the instability of sub-Saharan Africa that is added to the instability of North Africa. But let's move on. Brexit has deprived the European Union of one of its most important members, with military force and atomic weapon. The Brexit and the weakening of NATO at Trump's hands have opened a very deep wound in Europe's security system, even aggravated by European hesitations over Albania and Macedonia. The world is experiencing a huge health crisis and to contain it Italy is investing public resources above our possibilities. But this must not distract us from our underlying problems. Italy has long been plunged into a deep system crisis, a structural crisis that primarily affects our constitutional system, the very structures of parliamentary democracy and affects the productive world and society. And it is very difficult to govern the consequences of unforeseen and uncontrollable events such as the Covid-19 with a lost public system, without solid points of reference. However, the European Council's agenda tells us that the system crisis is far wider than Italy; it is a system crisis that even affects Europe, which seventeen years ago lost the opportunity to give itself a Constitution that would have laid the foundations for a true political union. And it is also a system crisis that makes the world order increasingly fragile, which after Yalta has no longer found a balance point. In this context, the fear that peace may also break is not a hollow thought. When historians describe the time we are living, they will not only speak of scientific progress and great transformations, but also of our indecisions, which, due to neglect or cowardice, are putting at risk much of the balance of peace and well-being that had been laboriously achieved. We had hoped that Brexit would be defeated in the referendum; today we are afraid of indifference to climate change and we are concerned about Europe's detached attitude towards Africa's evolution and the changing balance of the Mediterranean. We do not like that Trump considers the defence of Europe a burden for America and our economy a nuisance for the American markets, to be put in line with any means, including duties. All these factors shake the stability of the planet and have real consequences for the security of large parts of the world, including Europe, which has enjoyed seventy-five years of peace precisely because of that stability that has cracked today. The issue that the European Council's agenda implies is precisely that of the progressive destabilisation of the world order and the resulting dangers to world peace. Hence the urgent need to reopen a serious reflection on Europe's defence, knowing that when we finally achieve a true common defence of Europe, we will also have achieved a stronger political union and greater integration of the European continent. Italy repudiates war and works to ensure peace: in this spirit, in recent decades, the commitment to peace of the Italian armed forces has allowed Italian soldiers on a mission in the world to face daily situations at high risk and to achieve more efficiency, more modern vision and greater integration with colleagues from other nations. But that is not enough, because even Germany and France, which are two weighty countries, have on their own a military apparatus capable of guaranteeing European defence. I have gone a long way, Mr President, but it is necessary to say that the establishment of real European armed forces is an increasingly topical issue and that it is precisely the European Council that is the place where this issue can be put. Europe can no longer afford to neglect its defence needs. He can't do it at all, and he can't do it at such an indeterminate and confusing stage as the one the world is going through. I would therefore ask the President-in-Office of the Council to go to the European Council to remind us of Africa, the climate, Brexit, Azerbaijan, the Armenians and Nagorno-Karabakh, Albania and Macedonia, not to mention the dozens of regional or local wars taking place in the world and the trade wars and the technological primacy. All this tells us that we live a time of great instability, a difficult time, very aggressive, in which we need a long view and we need to know how to see the future. If we want peace, the defence of Europe must become a priority commitment of the Union and of all Europeans. [[Applause]]

Everything is in fact confused and veiledly terrorist in the way we are informed, starting from the emphasis placed on the concept of chance. A case in medicine usually indicates a sick person: a case of measles is one that has taken measles; but today it indicates mostly a healthy carrier, on whose contagiousness the scientific debate expresses doubts, while the talk shows pass of certainty in certainty. After all, we are in the country that considered the bodies contagious: we all remember that autopsies were forbidden. I mean, there's a technical-scientific committee asking us to do things that don't matter if they make sense. And this is the tool that you have authorized to discharge your responsibilities. ParlaMint-IT_2020-10-13-LEG18-Senato-sed-264.ana.u158	Mr President-in-Office of the Council, there are many issues at the next European Council, but before we go into detail and analyse some of them, I would like to make a brief premise. To these thanks and to this confidence we associate myself and all the Group of Movimento 5 Stars to the Senate [[Applause]] One of the topics to be addressed by the European Council is the relaunching of the partnership between the European Union and Africa on trade, development cooperation, security and the management of migration flows. The Cotonou Agreement, which has been contributing to the fight against poverty for 20 years, needs to be renewed. This is also an important agreement for the management of migration flows, as it works to improve conditions in countries of origin and transit, facilitates legal channels of migration and the repatriation of irregulars. The joint strategy between Africa and the European Union aimed at strengthening political cooperation between the European Union and the African Union, whose regular action plans stopped in 2017. The same need for revival applies to all three regional strategies, the Horn of Africa, the Gulf of Guinea and the Sahel. The latter is particularly important because it has as its main objectives the prevention and combating of radicalisation and the creation of appropriate conditions for young people, migration, mobility and border management, the fight against illicit trafficking and transnational organised crime. Mr President-in-Office of the Council, you have shown that you have a vision and a capacity for analysis that is not permeated by the current short thinking, the son of a liquid society, which is aimed only at an ephemeral and immediate consensus. Allow me, therefore, to give a critical thought. All these important common European initiatives must necessarily be accompanied by a Copernican revolution in the economic and financial relations between Europe and Africa, because I consider it unnecessary to cooperate on development with one hand if with the other we continue to tolerate forms of neocolonialism and predatory exploitation of the natural, financial and human resources of the African continent. I am not just talking about certain European multinationals that are plundering, polluting and impoverishing the lands and peoples of Africa, doing business with corrupt regimes or even worse by selling them weapons that fuel wars and humanitarian crises. I am also thinking of the European tax havens, where these corrupt African rulers hide almost a third of the wealth of the African continent, a theft that - I quote data from the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development and the United Nations Organization - every year takes away from the African tax between 50 and 100 billion dollars, that is the equivalent or twice the annual value of all international development aid that arrives in Africa. With one hand he gives himself and with the other he steals the future to the African peoples. Europe must change radically in this respect too, honouring its deep ethical and humanistic roots. Mr President-in-Office of the Council, at the next European Council there will be another subject which I personally consider to be extremely important and which is closely linked to the migration issue. I am referring to climate change caused by an unsustainable development model that creates pollution in our cities and that, in countries already experienced by poverty, causes desertification and other extreme climate phenomena that generate new flows of climate migrants. An ecological conversion based on a cultural revolution indispensable to preserve our country is a political imperative that cannot be postponed. We live on a planet with limited resources and it is also the time we have to intervene before it is too late. This government has already done so much: I am thinking of the bill saves the sea, the stop to drills, ecobonus and incentives to sustainable mobility. We are doing our part, but these global phenomena need global responses. So today more than ever, in the current historical context, Europe represents the most concrete possibility of modifying this paradigm that is likely to lead us to collapse. This is why the European green deal is a top priority, even taking into account the enormous employment and technological repercussions that this change could bring about. I am beginning to conclude by saying with conviction that you and your ministers in Europe are doing unprecedented work. You are converting a paradigm that had transformed the Community's dream of a Europe of peoples into a Darwinist nightmare. His political and diplomatic intelligence with our European partners has brought unprecedented results, unlike those who think they can get something by howling or childish threats. The 209 billion euros awarded - other than arithmetic calculation, as has been said! - are the result of intense work, I would say Carthusian, and represent a fundamental result for the future of our country, so wounded by the virus. But now, Mr President, we must continue to pursue the path of community sharing and sense. To think that we can solve everything with the becero nationalism, which we have also heard in previous speeches, is only shortsighted politics, which inevitably leads us to fall into a deep precipice. To counter these drifts they serve, instead, a healthy patriotism, farsightedness and ability to fly high; an objective that we can only achieve by freeing ourselves from zavorre such as ignorance, fear and that swindling mentality, which even in this difficult time we have seen staying in certain administrators. President-in-Office of the Council. - Mr President, I should like to end by wishing you good work, by renewing your call to move forward and by hoping that you will be able to bring this spirit of change to the next European Council. [[Applause.]]
